# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-18

## Recommendation System
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Encoded but Exposed: Language Embeddings can Reveal More Than They Should in Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21713v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Language embeddings are increasingly used in recommenders to represent item content, but their privacy risks remain poorly understood. We study whether item embeddings can reveal item identities when an adversary has access to auxiliary item text from external sources. We show that a simple cosine-similarity attack can recover item identities with high mean reciprocal rank across language models and candidate-set settings on two real-world datasets. Leakage is strongest when the adversary's partial information overlaps with the victim representation, but it also persists through semantic alignment between independently sourced descriptions. We further show that item leakage can propagate to user re-identification from sparse ratings. Finally, we evaluate obfuscated descriptions as a mitigation and find a clear leakage-utility tradeoff.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Quantum Machine Learning Approach to Content-Based Media Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21562v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We use quantum machine learning techniques to develop a content-based media recommender system and evaluate its performance on TV programmes using textual descriptions from one week of BBC iPlayer data. Grounded in the Quantum Natural Language Processing framework (QNLP), programme synopses are mapped to variational quantum circuits using their semantic structure. The parameters of these circuits are trained via a supervised pairwise similarity learning task. We benchmark the quantum recommender against a classical BiLSTM baseline trained on the same dataset and objective. While both models achieve comparable performance on the similarity learning task, the quantum model requires significantly fewer parameters (47k vs. 17.8M). When the BiLSTM is constrained to a matching parameter budget, its performance degrades substantially (by 3.63 percentage points in Mean Average Precision (MAP)), indicating that the structured quantum representation provides a highly parameter-efficient alternative in this setting. We demonstrate the feasibility of executing the trained models on quantum hardware by deploying the circuits on Quantinuum's System Model H2 (and accompanying emulator) using a SWAP-test-based protocol. The results confirm that the similarity signal is preserved under realistic, noisy conditions. Taken together, these findings highlight the potential of variational quantum circuits for recommendation tasks and represent a practical step toward their real-world deployment.</p></details> |  |
| **[RecSoup: Efficient Model Merging Approach for Controllable Multi-Objective Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21220v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recommender systems often need to balance multiple conflicting objectives. Controllable Multi-Objective Recommendation (CMOR) enables dynamic adjustment of objective trade-offs at inference time without retraining, adapting to shifting user or system requirements. Existing CMOR approaches typically rely on output-level reranking or parameter-generating modules, which either limit the scope of feasible trade-offs or incur substantial training and inference cost. Model merging provides a promising alternative by reusing multiple objective-specific models via parameter combination. However, its direct application to recommender systems is challenging due to the lack of a shared linear regime and cross-objective interference. To address these issues, we propose RecSoup, a lightweight two-stage approach for effective CMOR via specialized model merging. RecSoup first mitigates parameter interference across objectives through a pruning strategy that jointly considers objective-specific importance and cross-objective relevance. It then performs adaptive parameter merging via a learnable preference-to-coefficient mapping, allowing dynamic trade-off control at inference time without joint retraining or additional generative modules. Theoretically, we prove that RecSoup outperforms the parameter generation paradigm in effectiveness and efficiency, as it can fully navigate the Pareto front without smoothness constraints. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate that RecSoup achieves improved Pareto front quality and effective controllability while delivers 165--228x faster inference-time latency than parameter generation approaches. Code is available at https://github.com/35798642/RECSOUP.git.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at CIKM 2026</p></details> |
| **[From Clicks to Intent: Cross-Platform Session Embeddings with LLM-Distilled Taxonomy for Financial Services Recommendations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26277v3)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sequential user behavior modeling is widely adopted in industrial recommender systems; however, significant gaps remain in financial services, where pre-login web interactions and authenticated in-app experiences differ drastically. Specifically, pre-login web users typically explore new products, whereas logged-in app users focus on account servicing. Due to the challenge of cross-channel entity resolution (e.g., matching anonymous web sessions to authenticated mobile accounts), web-based intent signals remain underutilized for post-authentication personalization. Existing methods for capturing web-based intent are often ad-hoc and narrow, lacking the flexibility to support both quantitative downstream recommendations and qualitative understanding at scale. In this work, we propose a scalable and dual-purpose intent prediction framework for web-based interactions and demonstrate its applicability for personalization. Our approach transforms raw web clickstreams into two outputs: a self-supervised Transformer encodes multi-modal clickstreams into a compact session embedding, while an LLM-based taxonomy generation and distillation pipeline produces interpretable intent labels. Our system demonstrates that self-supervised clickstream representations combined with LLM-distilled taxonomies can jointly serve quantitative tasks and qualitative understanding in production: on the mobile homepage tile ranking task, the session embedding improves macro Recall@1 by 1.88% and reduces Log Loss by 13.38% over production baselines. On the user conversion prediction task, the embedding outperforms the LLM labels by 4.3% on micro F1, while the distillation layer delivers interpretable labels at ultra-low latency with only a 7% performance drop.</p></details> | <details><summary>Dianj...</summary><p>Dianjing Fan and Yao Li equally contributed to this work. 7 pages, 1 figure</p></details> |
| **[On the Impact of Graph Neural Networks in Recommender Systems: A Topological Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.07384v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In recommender systems, user-item interactions can be modeled as a bipartite graph, where user and item nodes are connected by undirected edges. This graph-based view has motivated the rapid adoption of graph neural networks (GNNs), which often outperform collaborative filtering (CF) methods such as latent factor models, deep neural networks, and generative strategies. Yet, despite their empirical success, the reasons why GNNs offer systematic advantages over other CF approaches remain only partially understood. This monograph advances a topology-centered perspective on GNN-based recommendation. We argue that a comprehensive understanding of these models' performance should consider the structural properties of user-item graphs and their interaction with GNN architectural design. To support this view, we introduce a formal taxonomy that distills common modeling patterns across eleven representative GNN-based recommendation approaches and consolidates them into a unified conceptual pipeline. We further formalize thirteen classical and topological characteristics of recommendation datasets and reinterpret them through the lens of graph machine learning. Using these definitions, we analyze the considered GNN-based recommender architectures to assess how and to what extent they encode such properties. Building on this analysis, we derive an explanatory framework that links measurable dataset characteristics to model behavior and performance. Taken together, this monograph re-frames GNN-based recommendation through its topological underpinnings and outlines open theoretical, data-centric, and evaluation challenges for the next generation of topology-aware recommender systems.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted for publication in Foundations and Trends in Information Retrieval</p></details> |
| **[Bridging the Topological Divide: Dual-Branch Graph Distillation with Semantic Priors for Robust Cold-Start Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19578v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph Collaborative Filtering models have achieved remarkable success in recommendation systems. However, they inherently suffer from a catastrophic representation collapse in strict cold-start scenarios due to the topological isolation of unobserved items. While integrating multi-modal semantic features offers a potential remedy, directly mapping isolated semantic attributes into a globally topology-driven collaborative space frequently precipitates representation degradation and optimization conflicts. To bridge this fundamental structural divide, we propose DualGCN, an innovative Dual-Branch Graph Knowledge Distillation Framework. Operating under a decoupled two-stage training paradigm, DualGCN first constructs a robust collaborative teacher network where item representations are anchored by a topology-smoothed semantic prior, effectively reshaping the latent manifold. Subsequently, a semantic student branch employs a residual mapping mechanism and a magnitude-agnostic cosine distillation objective to achieve high-fidelity cross-modal alignment without disrupting established collaborative signals. Furthermore, we introduce a dynamic norm reconstruction mechanism to rigorously calibrate the physical scales of novel and warm items during global retrieval. Extensive experiments across three real-world benchmark datasets demonstrate that DualGCN significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in strict cold-start scenarios while comprehensively preserving recommendation accuracy for highly active items.</p></details> |  |
| **[xLightGCN: Explainable Graph Neural Network Based Recommendation Systems with Most Influential Peers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19446v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recommendation systems, systems that predict a user's preference for items in order to curate that user's feed, are a ubiquitous and central feature in online social networks. While they are a central piece of our modern digital infrastructure, many modern approaches are opaque black boxes, making them difficult to audit, understand, and assess the trustworthiness of. In this paper we present an exact, graph-based approach for generating explanations for decisions made by recommendation systems. We argue that in the risk-sensitive sociotechnical context of user-facing AI such as recommendation systems, explainability is a key pillar for establishing the trustworthiness of models and validating their effectiveness. We propose a technique built on LightGCN like architectures, an influential and popular class of recommendation models. We describe our approach and argue that it provides an appropriate assurance mechanism for these AI systems leading to specific advantages, including interpretable explanations and a theoretically guaranteed measure of explanation fidelity. We conclude by discussing limitations and directions for future research.</p></details> |  |
| **[Prompt or Policy? Optimizing Physician Slate Presentations with LLM User Simulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18309v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Choosing the right physician is a key challenge for patients seeking medical care. Physician recommendation systems identify relevant doctors, but patients still find it difficult to compare their profiles to make a decision. We formulate slate presentation optimization: given a patient's medical case and a fixed, ranked list of physicians, we generate short, case-specific summaries that make doctors easier to compare, while remaining grounded in the information in their profiles. To optimize physician presentations without relying on real patient interactions, we develop an offline framework that uses persona-conditioned LLMs to simulate patient choices. We generate patient personas from attributes extracted from each medical case and evaluate presentations for factual accuracy, relevance, naturalness and simulated patient choice. We also use a second LLM judge, unseen during optimization, to assess simulator-specific overfitting. We compare automatic prompt-level optimization with GEPA, parameter-level optimization with GRPO, and their combination across two model sizes. GRPO substantially outperforms GEPA in satisfying the full set of constraints, and its gains in simulated conversion persist under the unseen judge. GEPA alone brings smaller improvements, but using its optimized prompt to initialize GRPO speeds up training and achieves the highest simulated conversion. These results show that prompt-level and parameter-level optimization play complementary roles in improving how physician recommendations are presented.</p></details> |  |
| **[SimInter: Evidence-Grounded User Simulation with Interview-Based Profile Enrichment for Conversational Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18308v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Conversational Recommender Systems (CRSs) require reliable user simulators for cost-effective evaluation. LLM-based simulators have made realistic simulation possible but typically condition on profiles derived from explicit ratings, demographics, and static sources capturing surface preferences while omitting implicit behavioral characteristics such as decision-making style, aspect-level preferences, and domain expertise. We propose SimInter, a pipeline that elicits these attributes via synthetic interviews between two LLM agents, grounded in the user's statistical profile and historical conversational evidence. Before profile enrichment, an audit agent explicitly flags potential fabrication and stereotyping at the turn level. We instantiate and evaluate SimInter on movie recommendation. We simulate 300 ReDial users and, on a matched subset of 100 users, compare SimInter with a statistics-only baseline: SimInter significantly increases the diversity of expressed preference aspects (filtered aspect entropy: 1.619 vs. 0.859; paired d = 0.96, Holm-adjusted p < 0.001). This gain persists when the simulator converses with two trained recommenders, KBRD and UniCRS, under two independent evaluation judges (+0.28 to +0.56 nats; all p < 0.001). Human evaluation finds 94.3% of enriched-attribute claims supported or partially supported, while human judgments agree with 95.2% of the auditor's binary filtering decisions on a stratified sample.</p></details> | <details><summary>25 pa...</summary><p>25 pages, 11 figures, 12 tables. Erfan Shahabi and Amirhossein Roshandel contributed equally</p></details> |
| **[LLMRec: Benchmarking Large Language Models on Recommendation Task](https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.12241v2)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recently, the fast development of Large Language Models (LLMs) such as ChatGPT has significantly advanced NLP tasks by enhancing the capabilities of conversational models. However, the application of LLMs in the recommendation domain has not been thoroughly investigated. To bridge this gap, we propose LLMRec, a LLM-based recommender system designed for benchmarking LLMs on various recommendation tasks. Specifically, we benchmark several popular off-the-shelf LLMs, such as ChatGPT, LLaMA, ChatGLM, on five recommendation tasks, including rating prediction, sequential recommendation, direct recommendation, explanation generation, and review summarization. Furthermore, we investigate the effectiveness of supervised finetuning to improve LLMs' instruction compliance ability. The benchmark results indicate that LLMs displayed only moderate proficiency in accuracy-based tasks such as sequential and direct recommendation. However, they demonstrated comparable performance to state-of-the-art methods in explainability-based tasks. We also conduct qualitative evaluations to further evaluate the quality of contents generated by different models, and the results show that LLMs can truly understand the provided information and generate clearer and more reasonable results. We aspire that this benchmark will serve as an inspiration for researchers to delve deeper into the potential of LLMs in enhancing recommendation performance. Our codes, processed data and benchmark results are available at https://github.com/williamliujl/LLMRec.</p></details> |  |
| **[Web Scale on a Budget: Tail-Aware Retrieval Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17986v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A common practice in instance retrieval is to grow the gallery size to improve a benchmark's discrimination capability. One prominent example is ILIAS, which adds up to 100 million distractor images. The prevailing assumption is that faithful evaluation demands web scale, and that indexing all 100M images through the backbone is the unavoidable price of a comparable number. That price is steep: hundreds of GPU-hours and ~200 GB of descriptors per model, paid again for every method or ablation. We show the web-scale indexing price is largely avoidable: evaluation itself obeys a scaling law. The number of distractors that outrank a positive is Binomial in the gallery size. We define the per-query retrieval hardness, a gallery-scale agnostic quantity that yields a retrieval metric at any scale, and demonstrate it can be derived from a small random sample. The underlying sampled-rank machinery is long established in recommender-system and biometric evaluation, but to our knowledge has never been adapted to visual retrieval. We make the law per-query and estimable, and validate it at true 100M scale on ILIAS and INSTRE, and on the full 759k-distractor Google Landmarks v2 pool. Concretely, within a 5% sample (a flat 20x less indexing), every backbone's tail-aware (EVT) estimate has converged onto the large-scale mAP, to within ~0.01 (mean absolute error 0.005), across five backbones, three benchmarks, and two distractor pools (mAP@1000 at 100M on ILIAS/INSTRE; the official mAP@100 at the full 759k pool on GLDv2); the recommender-systems count baseline over-predicts everywhere (+0.037 to +0.068). This is an order of magnitude better than naively reporting the metric on the same subset (+0.08 to +0.13). We release code and the per-query hardness to facilitate faster, cheaper, and greener retrieval evaluation.</p></details> | <details><summary>19 pa...</summary><p>19 pages, 8 figures, 10 tables</p></details> |
| **[Mitigating Popularity Bias in Sequential Recommendations with Sparse Prior-Regularized Objective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17823v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Popularity bias is a well-known challenge in recommender systems, often leading to the over-exposure of popular items and reduced catalog coverage. In this work, we propose PR-entmax, a sparse prior-regularized objective that makes popularity correction context-dependent. We derive our method from a generalized variational principle in which an item popularity prior is incorporated directly into a sparse probability mapping, yielding a popularity-aware active support: the set of items receiving non-zero probabilities and hence non-zero gradients is determined jointly by contextual relevance and item popularity. As a result, the prior affects not only item probabilities, but also which items participate in gradient updates during training. The method requires no architectural changes and can be integrated into existing training pipelines by modifying only the loss function. At inference time, the prior term is removed, and items are ranked by the learned contextual relevance alone. Experiments across multiple benchmark datasets and sequential recommendation architectures show that PR-entmax consistently reduces popularity bias while preserving or improving recommendation relevance, advancing the relevance-debiasing trade-off.</p></details> |  |
| **[Nonparametric Kernel Clustering with Bandit Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.07535v2)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Clustering with bandit feedback refers to the problem of partitioning the arms of an $N$-armed stochastic bandit according to their underlying distributions, based on noisy observations collected sequentially and adaptively. This setting has attracted growing interest for its applicability in recommendation systems, crowdsourcing, and adaptive trials. Existing works rely on the strong assumption that arm distributions are sub-Gaussian, which can only distinguish arms whose distributions differ in mean - missing realistic settings where distributional differences lie in shape or higher moments. % We introduce a framework of \emph{nonparametric clustering with bandit feedback}, free of any parametric assumptions regarding the arm distributions. We adopt a kernel-based approach and reformulate the problem as clustering arms according to their kernel mean embeddings in a reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS). Building on this formulation, we introduce the \kabc\ algorithm, prove that it is $δ$-PAC, and derive a non-asymptotic high-probability upper bound on its sampling complexity. The bound is governed by a variance-aware signal-to-noise ratio defined as the minimum over pairs of arms in distinct clusters of the ratio between their squared MMD and their RKHS variance. \kabc\ achieves instance-dependent guarantees while being adaptive to this unknown ratio. We validate our approach empirically on synthetic and real-world datasets, demonstrating that \kabc\ succeeds in distributional clustering settings where mean-based methods fail.</p></details> |  |
| **[FindRec: Stein-Guided Entropic Flow for Multi-Modal Sequential Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.04651v3)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern recommendation systems face significant challenges in processing multimodal sequential data, particularly in temporal dynamics modeling and information flow coordination. Traditional approaches struggle with distribution discrepancies between heterogeneous features and noise interference in multimodal signals. We propose \textbf{FindRec}~ (\textbf{F}lexible unified \textbf{in}formation \textbf{d}isentanglement for multi-modal sequential \textbf{Rec}ommendation), introducing a novel "information flow-control-output" paradigm. The framework features two key innovations: (1) A Stein kernel-based Integrated Information Coordination Module (IICM) that theoretically guarantees distribution consistency between multimodal features and ID streams, and (2) A cross-modal expert routing mechanism that adaptively filters and combines multimodal features based on their contextual relevance. Our approach leverages multi-head subspace decomposition for routing stability and RBF-Stein gradient for unbiased distribution alignment, enhanced by linear-complexity Mamba layers for efficient temporal modeling. Extensive experiments on three real-world datasets demonstrate FindRec's superior performance over state-of-the-art baselines, particularly in handling long sequences and noisy multimodal inputs. Our framework achieves both improved recommendation accuracy and enhanced model interpretability through its modular design. The implementation code is available anonymously online for easy reproducibility~\footnote{https://github.com/Applied-Machine-Learning-Lab/FindRec}.</p></details> | Accepted by KDD 2025 |
| **[An Analysis of Role Semantics in Directed Dynamic Graph Modeling for Edge Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.23135v3)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Edge classification on directed dynamic graphs is critical for understanding evolving user-item interactions and behavioral patterns in recommender systems and social platforms. Existing dynamic graph architectures typically process these interactions using shared parameters for nodes with semantically distinct roles, including source, destination, query, and neighbor. While abundant supervision in tasks such as link prediction provides sufficient gradient signal for models to implicitly deduce role semantics and overcome this conflation, there are often far fewer labels available for edge classification. We hypothesize that in such regimes, the ability of models to implicitly separate roles is restricted, degrading downstream performance. To empirically investigate this relationship, we introduce an experimental framework for sequence-based models that explicitly disentangles functional roles using targeted embeddings and an asymmetric pretraining objective. By providing architectural priors for these roles, the framework serves as an evaluative vehicle to isolate and measure the resulting performance delta across a range of label budgets. Our empirical evaluation demonstrates that this explicit role disentanglement yields substantial and consistent relative performance gains over baselines. Furthermore, representational analyses reveal that the mechanism by which the framework provides these benefits is a bifurcation of source and destination latent spaces that improves linear separability.</p></details> |  |
| **[Tackling the Modality Inhibitance Effect with Wavelet-based Modality Integration in Multi-modal Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16229v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Harnessing rich modality features has recently gained prominence in multi-modal recommendation systems (MRSs). To delineate user modality preferences, existing works focus on modality fusion, which can be achieved via attentive summation or concatenation. In this work, we introduce the modality inhibitance effect - a phenomenon which limits existing MRSs when these models transit from dual-modal to tri-modal configurations resulting in significant performance degradation. In particular, apart from the modal-specific contamination encapsulated in the newly introduced modality, the inclusion of new features results in excessive informational redundancy, thereby severely impeding effective integration within current approaches. To address the modality inhibitance effect, we propose a new multi-scale modality wavelet (MMWave) multi-modal recommender, which aims to fully capitalize on essential modality features and suppress modal-specific noise. This is acheived by employing wavelet decomposition from a multi-scale (time \& frequency) perspective, ensuring that only essential (non-redundant) features of each modality are extracted. We then account for the latent associations between item modality features by designing a new structure-aware multi-modal graph learning module that captures high-order latent structural information pertaining to items. To modulate the relative contributions of modality features and achieve effective integration, we then propose a new adaptive modality regulator component that employs a new cross-weight integration strategy. Experiments conducted on four real-world datasets validate the efficacy of our proposed model. The source code for this work has been made publicly available at https://github.com/kennethorq/MMWave.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published in IEEE Transactions on Knowledge and Data Engineering (TKDE) 2026</p></details> |
| **[Heterogeneous Agentic Coevolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16078v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in self-improving LLM agents have demonstrated the potential of AI systems that drive their own intelligence. However, many domain-specific applications rely on specialized foundation models that possess strong domain expertise but remain dependent on human-designed improvements. Extending recursive self-improvement to these models presents a key challenge: as a specialist improves, the strategies used to advance it must also adapt. To address this challenge, we propose Heterogeneous Agentic CoEvolution (HACE), a framework that jointly evolves specialized foundation models and LLM agents through reciprocal learning. HACE improves the specialist through supervised learning while training the agent with on-policy reinforced self-training, guided by verified performance gains on the current specialist. The agent's exploration, in turn, informs subsequent specialist training, enabling both models to adapt to each other's progress. To sustain this reciprocal learning, HACE further employs evolutionary search to optimize their interaction harness, adapting how the models exchange information and select training experiences as their capabilities evolve. We evaluate HACE across three challenging domains: recommendation systems, protein function prediction in biology, and crystal stability prediction in material discovery. We show that HACE outperforms the evaluated self-improving methods on the primary task metrics across all three domains. These results shed light on heterogeneous coevolution as a universal approach to improving specialized foundation models through reciprocal interaction with a learning agent.</p></details> |  |
| **[Local Citation Recommendation Without Graph Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15832v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Resolving correct citations can be a time-consuming task for research writers. We address local citation recommendation (LCR), where the goal is to retrieve appropriate references for a given sentence. Web tools generally depend on the existing citation graph, which may bias results toward citation count or more recent publications. LCR research using full paper texts has been limited by the lack of publicly available data. We present a new dataset, the Local Citation Recommendation Corpus (LCRC), containing 14,735 scientific statements derived from astrophysics review papers, each with one or more citation targets whose full text is accessible via API. Using this dataset we systematically compare retrieval methods and develop a citation recommendation system that avoids citation graph bias while achieving 8.0% improvement in Recall@25 and 6.9% improvement in NDCG@25 over dense retrieval baselines without model fine-tuning. To support reproducibility, we make our code and dataset publicly available.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at WASP 2026, co-located with AACL-IJCNLP 2026. 16 pages, 3 figures, 2 tables. Dataset: https://huggingface.co/datasets/adsabs/LCRC Code: https://github.com/benjaminb/citeline</p></details> |
| **[UNISON: A Unified Framework for Learning from Scored Bags](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15564v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce UNISON, a unified framework that bridges multiple instance learning and recommendation systems by treating both as instances of learning from scored bag collections of (item, score) pairs. Unlike traditional approaches that address bag-level classification and personalized recommendation as separate problems, UNISON decomposes learning into three interconnected stages: (1) learning universal item representations, (2) modeling bag-specific preference patterns through lightweight personalized parameters, and (3) inferring bag-level characteristics from learned preferences. We justify the architectural choice of linear bag-specific scoring through a first-order analysis: when item embeddings are sufficiently smooth in the score, a linear function approximates the score up to a Taylor remainder controlled by the curvature of the underlying preference function. For cold-start scenarios, we show that the cold-bag fitting problem reduces to ridge-less linear regression with a closed-form solution, and provide convergence guarantees for the iterative variant. Empirically, UNISON matches or outperforms state-of-the-art approaches across diverse domains: entertainment (books, movies), immunology (T cell receptor (TCR) based classification), and microbiome, offering superior computational efficiency and interpretability. A controlled synthetic experiment further confirms that the bag-specific parameters ($w_j, b_j$) recover the latent factors that drive item scores even when the projection $φ$ has no access to the original scoring mechanism (AUC > 0.998). UNISON is available at https://github.com/louzounlab/UNISON.</p></details> |  |
| **[Federated Semantic Residuals for Multimodal Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15505v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Federated Recommendation Systems (FedRSs) learn collaborative scores from interaction histories on device and rank items by them, so a higher score means a higher rank. Item text and images add semantic information that can reorder the list. Existing Federated Multimodal Recommenders (FedMRs) fuse this content into their models, which can obscure how content reorders the list. We therefore propose the SEmantic Residual Adapter (SERA), which keeps content out of an existing FedRS and adds semantic residual scores to its collaborative scores. Content can then lift an item above another only when its semantic residual score exceeds the other's by more than their collaborative score gap. This score grows with the item's content similarity to the user's interaction history and is capped at a residual bound. We prove that for any content, an item can enter the list only if its collaborative score is within twice this bound of the lowest one in the list. A larger bound lets content lift more items, including irrelevant ones, so the bound trades off how far content reaches against accuracy. With a validated bound, SERA improves all three FedRSs on all six datasets without adding personalized parameters. It outperforms FedMRs in most comparisons and lifts some FedRSs above centralized baselines.</p></details> | <details><summary>23 pa...</summary><p>23 pages, 14 figures. Includes appendices</p></details> |
| **[Toward Explainability in Multimodal Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15484v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal recommender systems effectively alleviate data sparsity and cold-start problems by integrating heterogeneous information, yet complex cross-modal representations and model decision-making processes also introduce new interpretability challenges. Despite the rapid growth of research on the interpretability of multimodal recommendation in recent years, relevant methods and evaluation criteria remain relatively fragmented, lacking a unified conceptual framework. To address this gap, this paper categorizes existing methods into modality explanation and post-hoc explanation based on the timing and manner of explanation generation, and further constructs a progressive system for each category. Building on this foundation, this paper synthesizes four categories of evaluation metrics from both objective quantitative and subjective perceptual perspectives, and reviews relevant datasets along the dimensions of application domain and data source. Through a comprehensive analysis of existing research, we further propose future research directions. This paper aims to establish a unified analytical perspective across multimodal representation, recommendation decision-making, and user preferences, providing a research foundation for building transparent and trustworthy multimodal recommender systems.</p></details> | <details><summary>19 pa...</summary><p>19 pages, 2 figures, 4 tables</p></details> |
| **[An LLM-powered Agentic Recommendation System for Connected TV Content Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09988v4)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recommendation systems, from traditional multi-stage to recent unified generative architectures, face challenges in incorporating diverse contextual signals, such as trending topics, breaking news, cultural events, and cross-surface user activities, into their ranking pipelines. These systems are designed to consume structured behavioral signals with consistent schemas, and lack the reasoning capability to naturally process unstructured or heterogeneously formatted contextual information. Incorporating such signals typically requires feature engineering, bespoke data pipelines, and carefully tuned heuristics. In this paper, we present an LLM-powered agentic recommendation system designed for Connected TV (CTV) content discovery that addresses these limitations. Our system leverages the reasoning capabilities of large language models to naturally process and synthesize diverse signals across varying schemas and structures, eliminating much of the manual integration inherent in traditional ranking and retrieval systems. Recognizing that current LLM-based solutions still fall short of traditional machine learning models in several recommendation tasks, including retrieval efficiency, personalization precision, and scalability, we adopt an agentic architecture that orchestrates specialized components, allowing each sub-task to be handled by the most suitable method, whether LLM-based or traditional ML. The main contribution of this work is our engineering approach to successfully overcoming the practical limitations of enabling LLM for recommendation, particularly inference latency. We share insights from our work and discuss the trade-offs and lessons learned in building a hybrid system that combines the flexibility of LLMs with the performance of established recommendation techniques.</p></details> | <details><summary>Best ...</summary><p>Best Paper at USRW @ RecSys 2026</p></details> |
| **[MILP the Gap: An Automated Reasoning Approach to Condorcet's Paradox](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.19851v2)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Computational social choice studies how conflicting individual preferences are aggregated into a collective decision, with applications to elections, AI evaluation/alignment, and recommendation systems. One measure of the (in)stability of the outcome is the number of voters who prefer an unchosen alternative to all alternatives in the committee. If this number is less than half the electorate, the committee is a Condorcet winning set, which may not exist if $k \leq 2$. More generally, there exist instances where any committee of size $k$ has at least a $\frac{2}{(k+1)}-ε$ fraction of the electorate preferring another alternative, for arbitrarily small $ε$. While recent work has made remarkable progress narrowing the gap, it is currently unknown whether this lower bound is tight. We develop a mixed-integer linear programming framework that computes exact bounds for fixed parameter values. Our approach depends only on $m$ and $k$; by modeling electorates as distributions over rankings, we eliminate any dependence on $n$. | **[Recommendation Systems for Exploratory Data Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39412v2)** | 2026-10-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A large class of data-centric tasks is exploratory, where users iteratively steer workflows, refining subjective goals as new insights emerge. These Exploratory Data Tasks (EDTs) are performed by millions of users with varying levels of expertise to understand unfamiliar data, discover trends, and identify evidence that informs critical decision-making. However, a key challenge in EDTs is the enormous space of possible actions that one can take at each step: users struggle to choose among thousands of joins, transformations, and aggregations, causing "exploration paralysis". Because EDT workflows are interconnected, each choice impacts subsequent exploration, and suboptimal choices can lead to inefficiency, missed insights, confirmation bias, and incomplete coverage. This calls for intelligent recommendations that efficiently guide users toward optimal EDT actions. We envision recommendation as a core capability of data systems, proactively guiding users toward promising actions and thereby lowering the barrier to exploratory data tasks. EDT recommendation is challenging because the action space is combinatorial and actions are data-dependent, which require costly materialization. Moreover, recommendation often involves bundles or sequences of actions across interdependent tasks, requiring coordination across tasks. In this paper, we present our vision of EDT recommendation systems along two axes: single-task vs. multi-task settings and single-action vs. multi-action recommendations. We outline a research agenda that progresses from recommending individual EDT actions to constrained bundles and sequences of actions, and ultimately to coordinated recommendations across interconnected EDTs. We identify research directions for incorporating various contexts (user, data, task, and ecosystem), addressing efficiency challenges, and coordinating across tasks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Route What Remains: A Meta-Modal Agent for Missing-Modality Candidate Reranking in Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25007v2)** | 2026-10-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Missing-modality recommenders usually reconstruct absent representations, although the observed evidence may not determine the missing content. | **[Two-Sided State-Space Models for Sequential Recommendation with Non-Random Multimodal Review Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00165v2)** | 2026-09-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Two-sided digital platforms are inherently dynamic: user preferences shift, item popularity evolves, and reviews both reflect and drive these changes. | **[KUAISHOU Explorer LLM-Rec Challenge 2026: Reasoning Generative Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39828v1)** | 2026-09-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generative recommendation, has been attracted a surge of attentions in industrial and academic research community, towards to build more smart system to build next-generation recommender. | **[Towards More Efficient, Robust, Instance-adaptive, and Generalizable Sequential Decision making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.09192v5)** | 2026-09-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The primary goal of my Ph.D. study is to develop provably efficient and practical algorithms for data-driven sequential decision-making under uncertainty. My work focuses on reinforcement learning (RL), multi-armed bandits, and their applications, including recommendation systems, computer networks, video analytics, and large language models (LLMs). Sequential decision-making methods, such as bandits and RL, have demonstrated remarkable success - ranging from outperforming human players in complex games like Atari and Go to advancing robotics, recommendation systems, and fine-tuning LLMs. Despite these successes, many established algorithms rely on idealized models that can fail under model misspecifications or adversarial perturbations, particularly in settings where accurate prior knowledge of the underlying model class is unavailable or where malicious users operate within dynamic systems. These challenges are pervasive in real-world applications, where robust and adaptive solutions are critical. Furthermore, while worst-case guarantees provide theoretical reliability, they often fail to capture instance-dependent performance, which can lead to more efficient and practical solutions. Another key challenge lies in generalizing to new, unseen environments, a crucial requirement for deploying these methods in dynamic and unpredictable settings. To address these limitations, my research aims to develop more efficient, robust, instance-adaptive, and generalizable sequential decision-making algorithms for both reinforcement learning and bandits. Towards this end, I focus on developing more efficient, robust, instance-adaptive, and generalizable for both general reinforcement learning (RL) and bandits.</p></details> | Ph.D. Thesis |

The observation model inherits the logistic bandit challenge of handling the problem-dependent constant $κ$, which accounts for the non-linearity of the link function and can grow arbitrarily large. To address these limitations, we consider general reward function classes and introduce the \emph{locally sensitive eluder dimension}, a novel complexity measure tailored to the logistic structure of preference feedback that yields fine-grained regret guarantees without unfavorable dependence on $κ$. We establish a first-order regret bound that, in contrast with what previous results suggest, demonstrates that learning with preference feedback is as statistically efficient as learning from direct reward observation. To resolve item collisions, GEAR integrates a context-conditioned reranking head into the generative process, efficiently disambiguating colliding items with minimal computational overhead. TIDE treats memory as a capacity-constrained population of experiences: temporal and semantic credit assignment estimates contextual fitness, while responsibility credit distributes outcome signals according to the memories referenced during generation. No comparable metric-sensitive guarantee is known for the transportation algorithms of Arndt et al.\ or Harada and Itoh. | **[FairDiff: Mitigating the Self-Reinforcing Matthew Effect in Diffusion Recommender Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36671v1)** | 2026-09-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While the "Matthew Effect" and filter bubbles are widely recognized outcome-level biases in recommender systems, we reveal that Diffusion Recommender Models (DRMs) uniquely compound this issue through their generative dynamics. ## Representation Learning
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Language Models Struggle to Use Representations Learned In-Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.04212v3)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Though large language models (LLMs) have enabled great success across a wide variety of tasks, they still appear to fall short of one of the loftier goals of artificial intelligence research: creating an artificial system that can adapt its behavior to radically new contexts upon deployment. One important step towards this goal is to create systems that can induce rich representations of data that are seen in-context, and then flexibly deploy these representations to accomplish goals. Recently, Park et al. (2024) demonstrated that current LLMs are indeed capable of inducing such representation from context (i.e., in-context representation learning). The present study investigates whether LLMs can use these representations to complete simple downstream tasks. We first assess whether open-weights LLMs can use in-context representations for next-token prediction, and then probe models using a novel task, adaptive world modeling. In both tasks, we find evidence that open-weights LLMs struggle to deploy representations of novel semantics that are defined in-context, even if they encode these semantics in their latent representations. Furthermore, we assess closed-source, state-of-the-art reasoning models on the adaptive world modeling task, demonstrating that even the most performant LLMs cannot reliably leverage novel patterns presented in-context. Overall, this work seeks to inspire novel methods for encouraging models to not only encode information presented in-context, but to do so in a manner that supports flexible deployment of this information.</p></details> |  |
| **[Motion- and Gravity-Aware Feedforward 3D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22177v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Feed-forward transformer models have recently achieved strong performance in multi-view 3D reconstruction, but they still rely primarily on appearance-based attention and often struggle in dynamic scenes with weak geometric cues. In this work, we introduce a motion- and gravity-aware framework under the Learning Using Privileged Information (LUPI) paradigm. During training, residual optical flow and gravity direction are provided as privileged signals to guide geometric representation learning; during inference, the model uses standard visual inputs only. We integrate these priors into transformer backbones with lightweight architectural changes, preserving feed-forward efficiency. Experiments on standard benchmarks show consistent gains in reconstruction accuracy and robustness, especially in scenes with dynamic objects, ambiguous structure, and challenging camera motion. Our analysis further shows that the optimal injection stage is backbone-dependent: middle-layer integration is generally preferable for VGGT, while late-stage integration is more effective for π3. These results suggest that LUPI offers a practical way to inject motion and physical priors into feed-forward 3D reconstruction without increasing test-time complexity.</p></details> |  |

Each node in an EHR graph encodes feature identity, observed value, and temporal position of clinical events. In this study, we systematically investigate the spectral constraints of unsupervised time-domain feature extraction across three autoencoder architectures (LSTMVAE, VAEEG, Transformer) and a state-of-the-art EEG foundation model. Using synthetic experiments and empirical validation, we show that while autoencoders capture macro-scale shifts such as alpha slowing, they remain biased against higher frequencies due to a magnitude-driven masking effect induced by the Mean Squared Error loss under 1/f-decaying spectra. Increasing latent dimensionality mitigates this attenuation but sacrifices latent compactness and generative stability. We argue that, as the field advances toward large-scale foundation-level feature extractors, it is imperative to move beyond simple time-domain reconstruction and to integrate spectral-domain loss terms and gradient-balancing techniques, so that the subtle high-frequency transients critical for diagnosing complex brain disorders are preserved in the latent representation.</p></details> |  |
| **[PulseBound: Future-Beat State Forecasting Under an Explicit Information Boundary](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12010v2)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Predictive representation learning from photoplethysmography (PPG) can violate causal information access even with causal attention, as normalization, nonlocal transforms, or companion views may depend on withheld samples. We introduce PulseBound, a PPG representation learner combining physiologically structured future-beat prediction with an explicit stored-window information boundary. A content-independent cutoff separates the visible prefix from the prediction target. Prefix-only normalization, suffix replacement before derived-view construction, and aligned masking ensure that encoder inputs depend only on the visible prefix and cutoff. This yields stored-suffix invariance: with fixed model state, randomness, prefix, and cutoff, changing the stored suffix cannot change the forecast context. A shared horizon-conditioned head predicts nine rhythm and morphology descriptors for up to four extractor-valid future beats, using elementwise validity masks; optional ECG-derived pulse-arrival-time supervision is restricted to training. On MIMIC and VitalDB groups held out from PulseBound backbone pretraining, PulseBound reduces nine-state transformed-space MAE relative to last-visible-beat persistence by 28.06% and 22.22%, respectively, with gains in MAE, MAE-Skill, and Spearman correlation across all 40 source-cutoff-horizon cells. In a separate comparison of seven models on 13 downstream tasks, PulseBound achieves the best mean on nine frozen linear-probe and seven full-fine-tuning tasks. Stored-suffix interventions cause zero recorded changes in forecast contexts or predictions, with zero suffix-input gradients at audited precision under the stored-window interface. | **[From Simulated Fluids to Living Tissue: Zero-Shot Foundation Model Transfer to Perturbed Embryos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16236v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Morphogenesis is driven by the interplay of genetic regulation and tissue mechanics. However, at tissue scales, the dynamics of Drosophila can be described as a continuum flow, motivating the usage of representations learned across other physical systems as priors for developmental dynamics. We evaluate autoregressive prediction both in-distribution and zero-shot under unseen temperature and genetic perturbations. Zero-shot refers to evaluation on perturbation conditions never seen during wild-type finetuning, without any additional training. In-distribution, a Fourier Neural Operator model trained from scratch achieves best performance on VRMSE. However, Walrus (CRPS) is the strongest model under the most severe distribution shift of unseen samples of mutated embryos and temperature conditions. Interestingly, predictions capture the shape of the mean surface tissue velocity trajectory and its separation between 17°C and 27°C, including the timing of the peak at Germ Band Extension onset, a biologically meaningful milestone of embryo development. Our results provide preliminary support that representations learned from different dynamical systems can transfer to experimental morphogenesis in developmental regimes, provided they admit a continuum description. | **[VGGA: Geometry-Grounded Autoencoder for Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16204v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autoencoders are the core component of latent video generative models, compressing high-dimensional videos into compact latent spaces for downstream generation. Building on this insight, we propose \textbf{V}ideo \textbf{G}eometry \textbf{G}rounded \textbf{A}utoencoder (\textbf{VGGA}), a geometry-aware video autoencoder that explicitly incorporates geometric constraints into the reconstruction process. Specifically, VGGA grounds the learned representations along three complementary geometric dimensions: \emph{Structural Depth}, \emph{Motion Correspondence}, and \emph{Projective Warping}. These objectives are unified within a Video Geometry Decoder, which employs a shared spatio-temporal upsampling trunk to jointly optimize geometric learning and RGB reconstruction. Through these geometry-grounded objectives, VGGA encourages the latent representations to capture the underlying 3D structure and motion of dynamic scenes. VGGA consistently improves video generation across both geometry-centric and human-motion domains, reducing gFVD by 28.1\% on RealEstate10K and 37.7\% on Taichi-HD. Notably, these substantial generation improvements are achieved while maintaining comparable reconstruction quality, demonstrating that geometry-grounded representation learning yields a more effective latent space for video generation.</p></details> |  |
| **[Sera: Semantic Representation Aggregation with Knowledge and LLM for Reliable and Interpretable Battery Health Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11567v3)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Battery state of health (SoH) forecasting is important for battery management, but remains challenging due to nonlinear degradation and heterogeneity across batteries. Experiments on the mainstream benchmark across multiple prediction horizons and different temporal models show that Sera consistently improves forecasting performance, achieving up to a 37.3% reduction in prediction error over the temporal baseline and enhanced generalizability. The results show that prediction responses are consistent with the meanings of key degradation descriptors across tested horizons. | **[Multi-Marginal Inverse Optimal Transport for Contrastive Learning Via Explicit Anchor-Positive-Negative Coupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33741v2)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Inverse Optimal Transport (OT) based methods for representation learning learn representations such that the global OT coupling between a pair of data marginals in the representation space, concentrates on the positive pairs. However, these methods $\textit{DO NOT}$ utilize negative pairs and hence are not truly contrastive in their approach. We show that this leads to issues of dimensional collapse and hence degraded downstream performance. To alleviate this, we develop a novel multi-marginal (MM) inverse OT (IOT) contrastive learning (CL) approach called Neg-MMIOT-CL, which learns representations such that the global multi-marginal OT (MMOT) coupling between a triple of data marginals, with respect to a carefully designed ground-cost between triplets of data points in the representation space, concentrates on the anchor-positive-negative $\textit{triplets}$. For a latent class model, we empirically show that Neg-MMIOT-CL alleviates dimensional collapse. Furthermore, for a specific choice of ground cost for all triplets in representation space, we prove that the optimal representation configuration for Neg-MMIOT-CL exhibits equiangular property for within-class and across-class representations, which translates to Neural-Collapse when the representation dimension is larger than the number of classes minus one -- a result that is $\textit{previously established only}$ for pairwise contrastive learning methods. Finally, we propose Neg-IOT-CL-PushPull, that is a computationally efficient alternative to Neg-MMIOT-CL, alleviating the high cost of computing MMOT plans needed during implementation. We formulate representation learning as Distribution Matching (DM), learning an augmentation-invariant encoder whose push-forward law matches an explicit reference law. Within DM, generative learning and representation learning can be viewed as directionally inverse distributional processes: generative learning maps a tractable reference to data, whereas representation learning maps data to a designed reference law. For the Mallows-distance instantiation studied here, we connect the population objective to class-centre separation and classification error and derive a finite-sample bound on downstream classification error. | **[Employing Discrete Fourier Transform in Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.06765v2)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Image representation learning via input reconstruction is a common technique in machine learning for generating representations that can be effectively utilized by arbitrary downstream tasks. The DFT provides meaningful global information at each frequency level, making individual frequency components useful as separate learning targets. When dealing with multidimensional input data, the DFT offers remarkable flexibility by enabling selective transformation across specific dimensions while preserving others in the computation. Moreover, certain types of input exhibit distinct patterns in their frequency distributions, where specific frequency components consistently contain most of the magnitude, allowing us to focus on a subset of frequencies rather than the entire spectrum. These characteristics position the DFT as a viable learning objective for representation learning. We validate our approach on the CIFAR-10 and STL-10 datasets under constrained training regimes. | **[FENAS: A Few-Shot Meta-Ensembling Framework for Efficient Neural Architecture Search with Synergized Zero-Cost Proxies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15307v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Efficient neural architecture search (NAS) requires accurate performance estimation under limited evaluation budgets. | **[What Really Works for Universal Multimodal Embeddings? A Controlled Study of Contrastive Training Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15035v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Contrastive learning has become the standard paradigm for multimodal representation learning, aligning semantically matched pairs while separating mismatched ones in a shared embedding space. Although various contrastive training strategies have been proposed across negative selection, batch construction, and gradient modulation, their actual effectiveness under controlled, reproducible conditions remains poorly understood. To address this, we systematically evaluate representative strategies under a unified benchmarking framework on MMEB. Surprisingly, direct evaluations reveal that most existing strategies offer marginal gains over a standard aligned baseline, with several even degrading overall performance. | **[PRISM: Precision Forcing for Generalist Vision-Language-Action Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14704v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Coupling a pretrained vision-language model (VLM) with a diffusion or flow-matching action expert has become the mainstream vision-language-action (VLA) paradigm for embodied policies, yet they remain unreliable once a task demands precise manipulation, e.g., submillimeter cable insertion. Along the generative path, inference integrates a locally supervised velocity field, so equally penalized errors at different flow times can affect the executed action unequally. Consequently, precision-task performance deteriorates as physical tolerance tightens by orders of magnitude. We call this supervision imbalance across contexts and flow times Flow-Time Starvation. | **[Prior-Informed Generalizable Low-Rank Representation Learning via Regularized Adversarial Reweighting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14414v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern learning problems increasingly leverage data from heterogeneous sources to improve robustness and generalizability under distribution shift, often with high-dimensional parameters admitting low-rank structure. ## Graph Transformers
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Size-Transferability of Graph Transformers with Convolutional Positional Encodings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.15239v4)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Transformers have achieved remarkable success across domains, motivating the rise of Graph Transformers (GTs) as attention-based architectures for graph-structured data. For any given graph state, this decides whether single-qubit Clifford gates reach every graph state in its LU class, and supplies a counterexample when they do not. We further show that expressiveness beyond 1-WL arises from input encoding rather than attention: random-walk positional encodings separate the constructed pairs, while additional constructions expose the limits of this remedy. | **[MxGPS: Multiplex Graph Transformers for a Power Grid Foundation Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13763v2)** | 2026-07-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Single-task fine-tuning of graph neural networks (GNNs) for power grid problems exhibits a systematic failure mode: models that achieve the lowest in-distribution error degrade the most under topology shift. | **[Evaluation Awareness Beyond English: Do LLMs Act Differently When Tested in Indic Languages?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22333v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) can often tell when they are being tested. This ability, known as evaluation awareness, can lead models to act more cautiously during safety evaluations than in real use. Existing work has studied evaluation awareness almost entirely in English, although models serve users in many languages. Indic languages are spoken by over a billion people, yet evaluation awareness remains almost entirely unstudied in them. We test four instruction-tuned models from three families in five Indic languages alongside English. A set of cues varies whether the model is told that it is monitored and whether the monitoring is a safety test. Models refuse harmful requests more often when they are told that they are being tested. This increase appears in all four models and all six languages. In Indic languages, refusal of harmful requests rises by 14.4 percentage points on average. The test cue also brings Indic refusal of harmful requests level with English, but raises refusal of benign Indic requests from 6.9% to 11.3%. We release all 47,880 judged responses to support further work. These results suggest that safety evaluations in Indic languages may not reflect how models treat their everyday users.</p></details> |  |
| **[Learning to Steer LLM Agents and Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22316v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM)-based agents and multi-agent systems increasingly tackle complex tasks through long-horizon tool use and inter-agent collaboration. In this work, we propose a steering agent that supervises execution on the fly and steers the system back on track when drift occurs. The steering agent observes the execution context, deciding when to intervene with a low-cost fast path and how to intervene with a high-cost slow path. To unlock its steering intelligence, the ability to assess goal drift and generate effective interventions, we jointly train the two paths with reinforcement learning, combining a pairwise ranking objective with group-relative policy optimization (GRPO). Experiments on information-seeking search tasks show that the steering agent provides effective supervision for both single-agent and multi-agent systems, improving their performance and efficiency. The learned steering intelligence generalizes to out-of-domain tasks, transfers to unseen executors, and supports controllable intervention frequency without retraining.</p></details> | 21 pages, 6 figures |
| **[Grid-Bench: Can LLMs Predict How Discrete Worlds Change?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22312v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Spatial instructions describe how to move objects, transform regions, or modify their surroundings. Given an initial grid, explicit rules, and an event history expressed as text, a model predicts the complete final grid. Reference execution makes every predicted value checkable, supporting comparisons across spatial operations, history lengths, and workloads. Evaluating six LLMs shows that complete-state accuracy falls sharply as histories grow, with substantial divergence among models that perform similarly on single events. Models with similar exact-match accuracy can nevertheless differ substantially in the number of incorrect cells on shared instances. Even over long histories, some models recover many correct changes beyond what copying the initial state would yield. Grid-Bench makes both complete recovery and these differences in returned states directly measurable across a range of spatial conditions.</p></details> | <details><summary>39 pa...</summary><p>39 pages, 15 figures, 15 tables, including appendices. Project website: https://grid-bench.github.io/ ; Code: https://github.com/grid-bench/Grid-Bench ; Data: https://huggingface.co/datasets/xzx34/Grid-Bench</p></details> |

| **[Librarian Agents: Training LLMs to Curate Long-Context Workspaces that Anticipate Future Queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22273v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reasoning over long document contexts remains a challenge for large language models (LLMs). A Bayesian multinomial GAM fitted to 44,527 true positives of the evaluative construction reveals previously undocumented genre-specific trajectories of change, enabling us to advance new hypotheses about the relationship between register formality and competing pressures of morphosyntactic reduction and enhancement. | **[LLM Benchmarking via Representation Multi-task Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05613v3)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Quantifying and evaluating the capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) remains a fundamental challenge in modern data science and artificial intelligence. | **[SoK: Trading Agents or Market Crashers? Across 15 academic schemes, none earns full credit on any robustness metric, no paper describes a way to halt trading without waiting for LLM inference, and none combines source provenance with cross-source corroboration. | **[When Do LLMs Apply the Wrong Law? Third, temporal-law hints also increase uncertainty during statutory-version reasoning, suggesting that they may help models reconsider their default preference for newer law. Cross-model average susceptibility is highest for framing at 33.2%, followed by anchoring at 23.5% and halo at 18.4%. Furthermore, models often change their verdict from safe to vulnerable based on the cognitive condition, without accurately identifying the actual vulnerability. In the pure-forward, communication-free, infinite-buffer setting, T(M) = (M-1)tau_max + Sum_j tau_j, where tau_j is stage j's per-micro-batch service time and M the micro-batch count (Theorem 1). These results quantify the GPipe overestimate, decompose the bias into a homogeneity term Sum_j (tau_max - tau_j) and a communication-ignoring term Sum_j d_j, and imply that balancing per-stage compute dominates communication reduction for steady-state throughput. A real system with partial overlap is sandwiched between Theorems 2 and 3, with a closed-form bound on the overlap throughput gain. | **[Evaluating Inquiry Learning in LLM-Based Agents: Hypothesizing, Reasoning, and Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21604v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scientific inquiry involves iterative cycles of exploration, hypothesis generation, and reasoning over observations to uncover relationships in complex systems. In this work, we introduce a framework for evaluating LLMs' inquiry capabilities in a unified, interactive setting. We extend the Countdown task to a mathematical environment where an agent probes the system by manipulating variables, collecting observations, and inferring relationships between them. This setup enables analysis of exploration strategies, causal discovery, and hypothesis refinement during inquiry. Across seven LLMs and multiple prompting strategies, our experiments reveal limited inherent inquiry capabilities and low success rates in discovering the underlying expressions. However, structured guidance inspired by educational scaffolding significantly improves performance. Our findings highlight current limitations of LLMs in strategic exploration and iterative hypothesis refinement, and show how simple guidance strategies developed for human learners in inquiry learning can support more effective inquiry behavior.</p></details> | <details><summary>EMNLP...</summary><p>EMNLP 2026 (Findings)</p></details> |

| **[SecureClaw: Clawing Back Control of LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.09549v3)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool-using large language model (LLM) agents face two distinct security failures: unauthorized external actions and exposure of sensitive plaintext inside the runtime before any final output check can intervene. Writes that change external state follow a PREVIEW$\rightarrow$COMMIT protocol in which only a trusted executor may execute the exact request authorized by policy. The runtime can still plan over summaries and reference IDs, but cannot directly resolve the IDs into secrets or perform side effects. Across the AgentDojo, AgentLeak, and Agent Security Bench (ASB) benchmarks, SecureClaw is the only defense we evaluate in a common harness that simultaneously retains usable task utility and achieves 0% attack success rate (ASR) on ASB, 0.64% ASR on AgentDojo, and 3.23% overall leak on AgentLeak's attacked parity lane, which measures final-output and internal-relay leakage. This version corrects the conference footer only; scientific content is unchanged</p></details> |

| **[Measuring Harness-Induced Belief Divergence in Multi-Step LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04528v3)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Software agents are becoming increasingly important in modern coding workflows, where they solve complex tasks through multi-step interaction with code repositories. A central component of these systems is the execution harness, which governs how the underlying LLM observes, acts on, and verifies its environment. We show that the harness can in turn shape the agent's evolving beliefs about the environment, even when the task, environment, and base LLM remain fixed. To reveal this effect, we introduce a belief-rollout diagnostic that traces how an agent's anticipated world state and future decisions evolve under alternative harnesses. We further separate immediate divergence introduced by the interface from divergence that develops over longer horizons. Across controlled coding tasks include HIBench-Code, SWE-bench Verified and Terminal-Bench, we find substantial cross-harness belief divergence that is often invisible from terminal success alone and can propagate into different downstream actions. Building on this observation, we introduce Belief-Invariant World-Modeling (BIWM), which exposes evidence hidden or compressed by harness execution and aligns the resulting belief trajectories across different harnesses. Our findings reveal that harness design can systematically shape how software agents understand and act on their environments, providing practical guidance for building and evaluating more reliable coding agents. | **[Bellman-Consistent Trajectory RL for LLM Arithmetic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21060v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>To address sparse terminal rewards in LLM arithmetic reasoning, we propose a Bellman-consistent trajectory RL method. | **[INCONVENIENT: A Benchmark to Assess LLM Generalization Capabilities from Convenience Samples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21038v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Machine learning models for tabular prediction typically assume that training data are representative of the population on which they will be deployed. In practice, however, data are often collected based on convenience, availability, or cost, resulting in systematic sampling biases that can distort feature--target relationships and undermine real-world generalization. Existing tabular distribution-shift benchmarks primarily construct out-of-distribution (OOD) settings by partitioning observations across naturally occurring domains. While such partitions may induce changes in P(Y | X), the resulting shifts are neither explicitly controlled nor isolated, and the evaluation distributions themselves are often not representative of the broader target population. We introduce INCONVENIENT, a benchmark designed to study robustness to convenience-sample bias. Rather than relying on naturally occurring domain partitions, INCONVENIENT directly perturbs feature--target relationships in the training distribution while preserving the original population distribution as the deployment environment. This setting more closely reflects real-world scenarios in which models are developed from unrepresentative samples and subsequently deployed on a broader population. The distinction is particularly relevant for large language models (LLMs), whose pretraining may encode population-level knowledge that extends beyond the relationships observed in a biased training dataset. Unlike existing distribution-shift benchmarks, INCONVENIENT provides explicit control over both the direction and severity of relationship shifts, enabling a systematic assessment of robustness to convenience sampling. The benchmark comprises 200 tabular classification and regression datasets spanning diverse application domains and generates around 15,500 shifted training distributions which lead to substantial downstream degradation.</p></details> |  |

At the cluster level, ADS organizes samples according to semantic patterns and adapts the inter-cluster sampling distribution to prioritize clusters within the policy's current reach. At the sample level, ADS tracks prompts near the policy's capability boundary within each cluster to obtain informative relative advantages. Experimental results across three LLMs and seven reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that ADS improves average accuracy by 5.5% over Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). Paired skill replacements against shared background rules are scored on the same feedback cases; least squares converts the score differences into relative skill values, and the top-ranked rules are retained, favoring incumbents on ties. On six MedCalc-Bench-Verified tasks with 120 held-out cases, CausalEvo achieves 60.00% accuracy, compared with 55.83% for SkillOpt and 51.67% without skills. Across the recorded training configurations, which use different optimization budgets, it consumes 55.6% fewer training tokens than SkillOpt. Gains concentrate in particular calculations alongside regressions on others, and paired accuracy intervals include zero. CausalEvo turns task feedback into controlled decisions about skill retention and replacement. To handle data larger than GPU memory, we extend TorchPlan with Dask-cuDF for on-demand partition loading on TPC-H SF100 with four H100 GPUs, achieving $2.54\times$ speedup. On MultiHopRAG model diversity (k=2), the repetition gap Rep=-8.33 pp [-13.25, -3.75] quantifies the difference between gains measured against Duplicate and Single-slot. On a matched FRAMES stronger-panel extension (n=200, k=3, aggregator output limit 4096), paired Delta Rep=+9.67 pp [+6.00, +13.50]; within panels, Rep changes sign (original -6.33; stronger +3.33). We formalize five operator questions (next-step inference, entry-to-crown-jewel pathing, combined-severity promotion, evidence provenance, and chokepoint identification) as reusable Cypher and Graph Data Science queries, plus a chain-severity score distinct from per-finding local severity.
